The sampling algorithm takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input, and outputs
2n
labels
\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
with
L_{i,\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}
for each
i\in[n]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.

\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},C,\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})\rightarrow(\widetilde{C},\mathsf{vk})
:

The garbling algorithm takes
1^{\lambda}
, a circuit
C\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
2n
labels
\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
as input, and outputs a garbled circuit
\widetilde{C}\in\mathcal{C}
and a verification key
\mathsf{vk}\in\mathcal{VK}
.

\mathsf{Eval}(\widetilde{C},\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})\rightarrow y
:

The evaluation algorithm takes
\widetilde{C}
and
n
labels
\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]}
where
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
as input,
and outputs
y
.

\mathsf{Del}(\widetilde{C})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes
\widetilde{C}
as input, and outputs a certificate
\mathsf{cert}\in\mathcal{D}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top

\mbox{\bf or }\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes
\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathsf{cert}
as input, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

We require that a certified everlasting garbling scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition 6.2 (Correctness for Certified Everlasting Garbling Scheme).

There are three types of correctness, namely, evaluation correctness, verification correctness, and modification correctness.

Evaluation Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
C\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y\neq C(x)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\widetilde{C},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},C,\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{Eval}(\widetilde{C},\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\widetilde{C},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},C,\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{Del}(\widetilde{C})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Modification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
and a QPT algorithm
\mathsf{Modify}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert}^{*})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\widetilde{C},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},C,\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})\\
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{q(\lambda)}\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{Del}(Z^{b}X^{a}\widetilde{C}X^{a}Z^{b})\\
\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Modify}(a,b,\mathsf{cert})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark
6.3.

Minimum requirements for correctness are evaluation correctness and verification correctness.
In this paper, however, we also require modification correctness,
because we need modification correctness for the construction of functional encryption in
Section

7.3
.

As security, we consider two definitions,

Definition

6.4

and

Definition

6.5
given below.
The former is just the ordinal selective security
and the latter is the certified everlasting security
that we newly define in this paper.
Roughly, the everlasting security guarantees that any QPT adversary with the garbled circuit
\widetilde{C}
and the labels
\{L_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]}
cannot obtain any information
beyond
C(x)
even if it becomes computationally unbounded after it issues a valid certificate.

Definition 6.4 (Selective Security for Certified Everlasting Garbling Scheme).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Samp},\mathsf{Grbl},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a certified everlasting garbling scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{selct}}(1^{\lambda},b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\mathsf{Sim}
be a QPT algorithm.

1.

\mathcal{A}
sends a circuit
C\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
and an input
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger computes
\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

If
b=0
, the challenger computes
(\widetilde{C},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},C,\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
, and returns
(\widetilde{C},\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})
to
\mathcal{A}
.
If
b=1
, the challenger computes
\widetilde{C}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},1^{|C|},C(x),\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})
,
and returns
(\widetilde{C},\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

4.

\mathcal{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. The experiment outputs
b^{\prime}
.

We say that
\Sigma
is selective secure if there exists a QPT simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
such that for any QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{selct}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\Expt{\Sigma,\cA}{\mathsf{selct}}(1^\secp,0)=1]-\Pr[\Expt{\Sigma,\cA}{\mathsf{selct}}(1^\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(72)

Definition 6.5 (Certified Everlasting Selective Security for Certified Everlasting Garbling Scheme).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Samp},\mathsf{Grbl},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a certified everlasting garbling scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathcal{A},\Sigma}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{selct}}(1^{\lambda},b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and an unbounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Let
\mathsf{Sim}
be a QPT algorithm.

1.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends a circuit
C\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
and an input
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger computes
\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

If
b=0
, the challenger computes
(\widetilde{C},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},C,\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
, and returns
(\widetilde{C},\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.
If
b=1
, the challenger computes
(\widetilde{C},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},1^{|C|},C(x),\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})
,
and returns
(\widetilde{C},\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

4.

At some point,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger, and sends the internal state to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

5.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
.
If the output is
\bot
, then the challenger outputs
\bot
, and sends
\bot
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\top
, and sends
\top
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

6.

\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

7.

If the challenger outputs
\top
, then the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is certified everlasting selective secure if there exists a QPT simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
such that for any QPT
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and any unbounded
\mathcal{A}_{2}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{selct}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\cA,\Sigma}{cert}{ever}{selct}(1^\secp,0)=1]-\Pr[\expb{\cA,\Sigma}{cert}{ever}{selct}(1^\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(73)

6.2
Construction

In this section, we construct a certified everlasting garbling scheme from a certified everlasting SKE scheme (
Definition

3.1
).
Our construction is similar to Yao’s construction of an ordinary garbling scheme
[
Yao86
]
, but there are two important differences.
First, we use a certified everlasting SKE scheme instead of an ordinary SKE scheme.
Second, we use XOR secret sharing, although
[
Yao86
]
used double encryption. The reason why we cannot use double encryption is
that our certified everlasting SKE scheme has quantum ciphertext and classical plaintext.

Before introducing our construction, let us quickly review notations for circuits.
Let
C
be a boolean circuit.
A boolean circuit
C
consists of gates,
\mathsf{gate}_{1},\mathsf{gate}_{2},\cdots,\mathsf{gate}_{q}
,
where
q
is the number of gates in the circuit.
Here,
\mathsf{gate}_{i}\coloneqq(g,w_{a},w_{b},w_{c})
, where
g:\{0,1\}^{2}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is a function,
w_{a}
,
w_{b}
are the incoming wires, and
w_{c}
is the outgoing wire.
(The number of outgoing wires is not necessarily one. There can be many outgoing wires, but we use the same label
w_{c}
for all outgoing wires.)
We say
C
is leveled if each gate has an associated level and any gate at level
\ell
has incoming wires only from gates at level
\ell-1
and outgoing wires only to gates at level
\ell+1
.
Let
\mathsf{out}_{1},\mathsf{out}_{2},\cdots,\mathsf{out}_{m}
be the
m
output wires.
For any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C(x)
is the output of the circuit
C
on input
x
.
We consider that
\mathsf{gate_{1}},\mathsf{gate_{2}},\cdots,\mathsf{gate}_{q}
are arranged in the ascending order of the level.

Our certified everlasting garbling scheme.

We construct a certified everlasting garbling scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cegc}}=(\mathsf{Samp},\mathsf{Grbl},\allowbreak\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})

from a certified everlasting SKE scheme

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cesk}}=\mathsf{SKE}.(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
(
Definition

3.1
).
Let
\mathcal{K}
be the key space of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cesk}}
.
Let
C
be a leveled boolean circuit.
Let
n,m,q
, and
p
be the input size,
the output size,
the number of gates,
and
the total number of wires
of
C
,
respectively.

\mathsf{Samp}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

For each
i\in[n]
and
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
, generate
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
\{L_{i,\sigma}\}_{i\in[n],\sigma\in\{0,1\}}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\sigma}\}_{i\in[n],\sigma\in\{0,1\}}
.

\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},C,\{L_{i,\sigma}\}_{i\in[n],\sigma\in\{0,1\}})
:

•

For each
i\in\{n+1,\cdots,p\}
and
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
, generate
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

For each
i\in[q]
,
compute

\displaystyle(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\widetilde{g}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{GateGrbl}(\mathsf{gate}_{i},\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{a}^{\sigma},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{b}^{\sigma},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{c}^{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}}),

(74)

where
\mathsf{gate}_{i}=(g,w_{a},w_{b},w_{c})
and
\mathsf{GateGrbl}
is described in Fig
1
.

•

For each
i\in[m]
,
set
\widetilde{d_{i}}\coloneqq[(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}^{0},0),(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}^{1},1)]
.

•

Output
\widetilde{C}\coloneqq(\{\widetilde{g_{i}}\}_{i\in[q]},\{\widetilde{d}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]})

and

\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[q]}
.

\mathsf{Eval}(\widetilde{C},\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]})
:

•

Parse
\widetilde{C}=(\{\widetilde{g_{i}}\}_{i\in[q]},\{\widetilde{d}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]})
and
\{L_{i,x_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]}=\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

•

For each
i\in[q]
,
compute
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{c}\leftarrow\mathsf{GateEval}(\widetilde{g_{i}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{a},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{b})
in the ascending order of the level, where
\mathsf{GateEval}
is described in Fig
2
.
If
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{c}=\bot
, output
\bot
and abort.

•

For each
i\in[m]
, set
y[i]=\sigma
if
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}=\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}^{\sigma}
. Otherwise, set
y[i]=\bot
, and abort.

•

Output
y\coloneqq y[1]||y[2]||\cdots||y[m]
.

\mathsf{Del}(\widetilde{C})
:

•

Parse
\widetilde{C}=(\{\widetilde{g_{i}}\}_{i\in[q]},\{\widetilde{d}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]})
.

•

For each
i\in[q]
, compute
\mathsf{cert}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{GateDel}(\widetilde{g_{i}})
, where
\mathsf{GateDel}
is described in Fig
3
.

•

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cert}_{i}\}_{i\in[q]}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

•

Parse

\mathsf{vk}=\{\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[q]}

and
\mathsf{cert}=\{\mathsf{cert}_{i}\}_{i\in[q]}
.

•

For each
i\in[q]
,
compute
\bot/\top\leftarrow\mathsf{GateVrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{cert}_{i})
, where
\mathsf{GateVrfy}
is described in Fig
4
.

•

If
\top\leftarrow\mathsf{GateVrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{cert}_{i})
for all
i\in[q]
, then output
\top
.
Otherwise, output
\bot
.

Gate Garbling Circuit
\mathsf{GateGrbl}

Input:

\mathsf{gate}_{i},\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{a}^{\sigma},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{b}^{\sigma},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{c}^{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}}
.

Output:

\widetilde{g_{i}}
and
\mathsf{vk}_{i}
.

1.

Parse
\mathsf{gate}_{i}=(g,w_{a},w_{b},w_{c})
.

2.

Sample
\gamma_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{S}_{4}
.
3
3

3

\mathsf{S}_{4}
is the symmetric group of order
4
.

3.

For each
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, sample
p^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}_{c}\leftarrow\mathcal{K}
.

4.

For each
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, compute
(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{a}^{\sigma_{a}},p^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}_{c})

and

(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{b}^{\sigma_{b}},p^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}_{c}\oplus\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{c}^{g(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})})
.

5.

Output
\widetilde{g_{i}}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\}_{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}}
in the permutated order of
\gamma_{i}
and

\mathsf{vk}_{i}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\}_{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}}
in the permutated order of
\gamma_{i}
.

Figure 1:
The description of
\mathsf{GateGrbl}

Gate Evaluating Circuit
\mathsf{GateEval}

Input:

A garbled gate
\widetilde{g}_{i}
and
(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{a},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{b})
.

Output:

\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{c}
or
\bot
.

1.

Parse
\widetilde{g_{i}}=\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\}_{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}}
.

2.

For each
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, compute

q_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{a},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})
and

q_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{b},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})
.

3.

If there exists a unique pair
(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
such that
q_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\neq\bot
and
q_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\neq\bot
, then
compute
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{c}^{{}^{\prime}\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\coloneqq q_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\oplus q_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}
and output
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{c}\coloneqq\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}_{c}^{{}^{\prime}\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}
.
Otherwise, output
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{c}\coloneqq\bot
.

Figure 2:
The description of
\mathsf{GateEval}

Gate Deletion Circuit
\mathsf{GateDel}

Input:

A garbled gate
\widetilde{g}_{i}
.

Output:

\mathsf{cert}_{i}

1.

Parse
\widetilde{g_{i}}=\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\}_{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}}
.

2.

For each
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, compute
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})
.

3.

For each
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, compute
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{CT}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{cert}_{i}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\}_{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}}
.

Gate Verification Circuit
\mathsf{GateVrfy}

Input:

\mathsf{vk}_{i}
and
\mathsf{cert}_{i}
.

Output:

\top
or
\bot
.

1.

Parse

\mathsf{vk}_{i}=\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\}_{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}}
and

\mathsf{cert}_{i}=\{\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}}\}_{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}}
.

2.

For each
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, compute
\top/\bot\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{a}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})
.

3.

For each
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, compute
\top/\bot\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{vk}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{cert}_{b}^{\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}})
.

4.

If all the outputs are
\top
, then output
\top
.
Otherwise, output
\bot
.

The description of
\mathsf{GateVrfy}

Correctness:

Correctness easily follows from that of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cesk}}
.

Security:

The following two theorems hold.

Theorem 6.6.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cesk}}
satisfies the IND-CPA security (
Definition

3.4
),

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cegc}}
satisfies the selective security (
Definition

6.4
).

Its proof is similar to that of
Theorem

6.7
,
and therefore we omit it.

Theorem 6.7.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cesk}}
satisfies the certified everlasting IND-CPA security (
Definition

3.5
),

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cegc}}
satisfies the certified everlasting selective security (
Definition

6.5
).

Its proof is given in
Appendix

B
.

7
Certified Everlasting Functional Encryption

In this section, we define and construct certified everlasting functional encryption (FE).
In
Section

7.1
, we define certified everlasting FE.
In
Section

7.2
, we construct a 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme with non-adaptive security for all
\textbf{{P}}/\textbf{{poly}}
circuits
from a certified everlasting garbling scheme (
Definition

6.1
) and a certified everlasting PKE scheme (
Definition

4.1
).
In
Section

7.3
, we change it to the adaptive one by using a certified everlasting RNCE scheme (
Definition

5.1
).
In
Section

7.4
, we further change it to a
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE for all
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
circuits
by using a multipary computation scheme.

7.1
Definition

Definition 7.1 (
q
-Bounded Certified Everlasting FE (Syntax)).

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and let
p
,
r
,
s
,
t
,
u
and
v
be polynomials.
Let
q
be a polynomial of the security parameter
\lambda
.
A
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme for a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions
is a tuple of algorithms

\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})

with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{n}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes p(\lambda)}
,
master public key space
\mathcal{MPK}:=\{0,1\}^{r(\lambda)}
,
master secret key space
\mathcal{MSK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{s(\lambda)}
,
secret key space
\mathcal{SK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{t(\lambda)}
,
verification key space
\mathcal{VK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{u(\lambda)}

and deletion certificate space
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes v(\lambda)}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})
:

The setup algorithm takes the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input, and outputs a master public key
\mathsf{MPK}\in\mathcal{MPK}

and a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}\in\mathcal{MSK}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{f}
:

The keygeneration algorithm takes
\mathsf{MSK}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
as input,
and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\rightarrow(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{MPK}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
as input,
and outputs a verification key
\mathsf{vk}\in\mathcal{VK}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT})\rightarrow y

\mbox{\bf or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and
\mathsf{CT}
as input, and outputs
y
or
\bot
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{CT})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes
\mathsf{CT}
as input, and outputs a certificate
\mathsf{cert}\in\mathcal{D}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top\mbox{ {\bf or} }\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes
\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathsf{cert}
as input,
and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

We require that a certified everlasting FE scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition 7.2 (Correctness for Certified Everlasting FE).

There are three types of correctness, namely,
evaluation correctness,
verification correctness,
and modification correctness.

Evaluation Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y\neq f(m)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{CT})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Modification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
and a QPT algorithm
\mathsf{Modify}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert}^{*})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{Del}(Z^{b}X^{a}\mathsf{CT}X^{a}Z^{b})\\
\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Modify}(a,b,\mathsf{cert})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark
7.3.

Minimum requirements for correctness are evaluation correctness and verification correctness.
In this paper, however, we also require modification correctness,
because we need modification correctness for the construction of certified everlasting FE in
Section

7.3
.

Remark
7.4.

In FE, we usually want to run
\mathsf{Dec}
algorithm for many different functions
f
on the same ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}
.
One might think that the quantum
\mathsf{CT}
is destroyed by
\mathsf{Dec}
algorithm, and therefore it can be used only once.
However, it is easy to see that
\mathsf{Dec}
algorithm can be always modified so that it does not disturb the quantum state
\mathsf{CT}

by using the gentle measurement lemma
[
Wil11
]
.

In this paper, we introduce four types of definitions of security,
Definitions

7.5
,
7.6
,
7.7
and
7.8
.
(We note that these securities are simulation based ones defined in
[
GVW12
]
.)
The first two definitions (
Definitions

7.5
and
7.6
) are ordinal security definitions of FE.
(
Definition

7.5
is non-adaptive one and
Definition

7.6
is adaptive one.)
The third and fourth definitions (
Definitions

7.7
and
7.8
) are security definitions with certified everlasting security that we
newly introduce in this paper.
(
Definition

7.7
is non-adaptive one and
Definition

7.8
is adaptive one.)

Definition 7.5 (
q
-Bounded Non-Adaptive Security for Certified Everlasting FE (Simulation Base)
[
GVW12
]
).

Let
q
be a polynomial of
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{non}\mbox{-}\mathsf{\mathsf{adapt}}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}
be a QPT algorithm.

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries at most
q
times.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
q^{*}
be the number of times that
\mathcal{A}
makes key queries.
Let
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}(m),f_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\}_{i\in[q^{*}]}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}
chooses
m\in\mathcal{M}
and sends
m
to the challenger.

4.

The experiment works as follows:

•

If
b=0
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger computes
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathcal{V},1^{|m|})
, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

5.

\mathcal{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. The output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
.

We say that
\Sigma
is
q
-bounded non-adaptive secure if there exists a
QPT
simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
such that for any
QPT
adversary
\mathcal{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{non}\mbox{-}\mathsf{adapt}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expa{\Sigma,\cA}{non}{\mathsf{adapt}}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expa{\Sigma,\cA}{non}{\mathsf{adapt}}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(88)

Definition 7.6 (
q
-Bounded Adaptive Security for Certified Everlasting FE (Simulation Base)
[
GVW12
]
).

Let
q
be a polynomial of
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{adapt}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Sim}_{2}
be a QPT algorithm.

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries at most
q
times.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
q^{*}
be the number of times that
\mathcal{A}
makes key queries.
Let
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}(m),f_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\}_{i\in[q^{*}]}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}
chooses
m\in\mathcal{M}
and sends
m
to the challenger.

4.

The experiment works as follows:

•

If
b=0
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{CT},\mathsf{st}_{q^{*}})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{1}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathcal{V},1^{|m|})
, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathsf{st}_{q^{*}}
is a quantum state.

5.

\mathcal{A}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries at most
q-q^{*}
times.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger works as follows:

•

If
b=0
, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
, computes
(\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}},\mathsf{st}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{2}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell},f_{\ell}(m),\mathsf{st}_{\ell-1})
, and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

6.

\mathcal{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. The output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
.

We say that
\Sigma
is
q
-bounded adaptive secure if there exists a
QPT
simulator
\mathsf{Sim}=(\mathsf{Sim}_{1},\mathsf{Sim}_{2})
such that for any
QPT
adversary
\mathcal{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{adapt}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\Expt{\Sigma,\cA}{\mathsf{adapt}}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\Expt{\Sigma,\cA}{\mathsf{adapt}}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(89)

Definition 7.7 (
q
-Bounded Certified Everlasting Non-Adaptive Security for Certified Everlasting FE (Simulation Base)).

Let
q
be a polynomial of
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{non}\mbox{-}\mathsf{\mathsf{adapt}}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and an unbounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Let
\mathsf{Sim}
be a QPT algorithm.

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries at most
q
times.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})

and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.
Let
q^{*}
be the number of times that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
makes key queries.
Let
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}(m),f_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\}_{i\in[q^{*}]}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
chooses
m\in\mathcal{M}
and sends
m
to the challenger.

4.

The experiment works as follows:

•

If
b=0
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathcal{V},1^{|m|})
, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

5.

At some point,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger and its internal state to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

6.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
.
If the output is
\top
, then the challenger outputs
\top
, and sends
\mathsf{MSK}
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\bot
, and sends
\bot
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

7.

\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. If the challenger outputs
\top
, the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is
q
-bounded certified everlasting non-adaptive secure if there exists a
QPT
simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
such that for any
QPT
adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and any unbounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{non}\mbox{-}\mathsf{adapt}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expd{\Sigma,\cA}{cert}{ever}{non}{\mathsf{adapt}}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expd{\Sigma,\cA}{cert}{ever}{non}{\mathsf{adapt}}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(90)

Definition 7.8 (
q
-Bounded Certified Everlasting Adaptive Security for Certified Everlasting FE (Simulation Base)).

Let
q
be a polynomial of
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{\mathsf{adapt}}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and an unbounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}_{1}
,
\mathsf{Sim}_{2}
, and

(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to

.

,

be the number of times that

\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}(m),f_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\}_{i\in[q^{*}]}

\mathcal{A}_{1}
chooses
m\in\mathcal{M}
and sends
m
to the challenger.

, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}

, the challenger computes

, and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, where
\mathsf{st}_{q^{*}}
is a quantum state.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries at most

times.
For the

-th key query, the challenger works as follows.

•

If
b=0
, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,

,
and sends

, computes

7.2
Construction of 1-Bounded Certified Everlasting Functional Encryption with Non-Adaptive Security

In this section, we construct a 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme with non-adaptive security from a certified everlasting garbling scheme (
Definition

6.1
) and a certified everlasting PKE scheme (
Definition

4.1
).

Our 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme with non-adaptive security.

We use a universal circuit
U(\cdot,x)
in which a plaintext
x
is hard-wired. The universal circuit takes a function
f
as input and outputs
f(x)
.
Let
s\coloneqq|f|
.
We construct a 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme with non-adaptive security
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})

from a certified everlasting garbling scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cegc}}=\mathsf{GC}.(\mathsf{Samp},\mathsf{Grbl},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
(
Definition

6.1
)
and a certified everlasting PKE scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}}=\mathsf{PKE}.(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
(
Definition

4.1
).

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

Generate
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
for every
i\in[s]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
and
f=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\coloneqq(f,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,f[i]}\}_{i\in[s]})
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{MPK}=\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

Compute
\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}\leftarrow\mathsf{GC}.\mathsf{Samp}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Compute
(\widetilde{U},\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{GC}.\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},U(\cdot,m),\{L_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
.

•

For every
i\in[s]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, compute
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha},L_{i,\alpha})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq(\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{vk},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})

and
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\widetilde{U},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=(f,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\}_{i\in[s]})
and
\mathsf{CT}=(\widetilde{U},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
.

•

For every
i\in[s]
, compute
L_{i},\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]})
.

•

Compute
y\leftarrow\mathsf{GC}.\mathsf{Eval}(\widetilde{U},\{L_{i}\}_{i\in[s]})
.

•

Output
y
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{CT})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{CT}=(\widetilde{U},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{GC}.\mathsf{Del}(\widetilde{U})
.

•

For every
i\in[s]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, compute
\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,\alpha}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq(\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{cert},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=(\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{vk},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
and
\mathsf{cert}=(\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{cert},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}})
.

•

Output
\top
if
\top\leftarrow\mathsf{GC}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{gc}.\mathsf{cert})
and
\top\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,\alpha})
for every
i\in[s]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.
Otherwise, output
\bot
.

Correctness:

Correctness easily follows from that of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cegc}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}}
.

Security:

The following two theorems hold.

Theorem 7.9.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cegc}}
satisfies the selective security (
Definition

6.4
) and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}}
satisfies the IND-CPA security (
Definition

4.4
),

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}
satisfies the 1-bounded non-adaptive security (
Definition

7.5
).

Its proof is similar to that of
Theorem

7.10
, and therefore we omit it.

Theorem 7.10.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cegc}}
satisfies the certified everlasting selective security (
Definition

6.5
) and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}}
satisfies the certified everlasting IND-CPA security (
Definition

4.5
),

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}
satisfies the 1-bounded certified everlasting non-adaptive security (
Definition

7.7
).

Its proof is given in
Appendix

C
.

7.3
Construction of 1-Bounded Certified Everlasting Functional Encryption with Adaptive Security

In this section, we change the non-adaptive scheme constructed in the previous subsection to the adaptive one by using
a certified everlasting RNC scheme (
Definition

5.1
).

Our 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme with adaptive security.

We construct a 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme with adaptive security
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})

from a 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme with non-adaptive security
\Sigma_{\mathsf{nad}}=\mathsf{NAD}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
,
where the ciphertext space is
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes n}
,
and a certified everlasting RNCE scheme

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathsf{cence}}=\mathsf{NCE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Fake},\mathsf{Reveal},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})

(92)

(
Definition

5.1
).
Let
\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Modify}
be a QPT algorithm such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert}^{*})\neq\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
a,c\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\\
\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Del}(Z^{c}X^{a}\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{CT}X^{a}Z^{c})\\
\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Modify}(a,c,\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

for any
m
.

Our construction is as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

Run
(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Run
(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{pk})
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MSK},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{MSK})
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MSK},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{MSK})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MSK},f)
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{MSK})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\coloneqq(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{sk})
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{MPK}=(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{pk})
.

•

Compute
(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{MPK},m)
.

•

Generate
a,c\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
\Psi\coloneqq Z^{c}X^{a}\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{CT}X^{a}Z^{c}
.

•

Compute
(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{CT})\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{pk},(a,c))
.

•

Output
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{vk},a,c)
and
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\Psi,\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{CT})
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{sk})
and
\mathsf{CT}=(\Psi,\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{CT})
.

•

Compute
(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{CT})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{CT}^{\prime}\coloneqq X^{a^{\prime}}Z^{c^{\prime}}\Psi Z^{c^{\prime}}X^{a^{\prime}}
.

•

Compute
y\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{CT}^{\prime})
.

•

Output
y
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{CT})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{CT}=(\Psi,\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{CT})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Del}(\Psi)
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{CT})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{cert})
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{vk},a,c)
and
\mathsf{cert}=(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{cert})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Modify}(a,c,\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert})
.

•

Output
\top
if
\top\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{cert})
and
\top\leftarrow\mathsf{NAD}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{nad}.\mathsf{cert}^{*})
.
Otherwise, output
\bot
.

Correctness:

Correctness easily follows from that of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{nad}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cence}}
.

Security:

The following two theorems hold.

Theorem 7.11.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{nad}}
satisfies the 1-bounded non-adaptive security (
Definition

7.5
) and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cence}}
satisfies the RNC security (
Definition

5.3
),

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}
satisfies the 1-bounded adaptive security (
Definition

7.6
).

Its proof is similar to that of
Theorem

7.12
, and therefore we omit it.

Theorem 7.12.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{nad}}
satisfies the 1-bounded certified everlasting non-adaptive security(
Definition

7.7
) and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cence}}
satisfies the certified everlasting RNC security (
Definition

5.4
),

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}
satisfies the 1-bounded certified everlasting adaptive security (
Definition

7.8
).

Its proof is given in
Appendix

D
.

7.4
Construction of
q
-Bounded Certified Everlasting Functional Encryption for
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
circuits

In this section, we construct a
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE for all
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
circuits from 1-bounded certified everlasting FE constructed in the previous subsection
and Shamir’s secret sharing (
[
Sha79
]
).
Our construction is similar to that of ordinary FE for all
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
circuits in
[
GVW12
]

except that we use a 1-bounded certified everlasting FE instead of an ordinary 1-bounded FE.

Our
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme for
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
circuits.

We consider the polynomial representation of circuits
C
in
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
.
The input message space is
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\mathbb{F}^{\ell}
, and for each
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
circuit
C
,
C(\cdot)
is an
\ell
-variate polynomial over
\mathbb{F}
of total degree at most
D
.
Let
q=q(\lambda)
be a polynomial of
\lambda
. Our scheme is associated with additional parameters
S=S(\lambda)
,
N=N(\lambda)
,
t=t(\lambda)
and
v=v(\lambda)

that satisfy

\displaystyle t(\lambda)=\Theta(q^{2}\lambda),N(\lambda)=\Theta(D^{2}q^{2}t),v(\lambda)=\Theta(\lambda),S(\lambda)=\Theta(vq^{2}).

(98)

Let us define a family
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\{G_{C,\Delta}\}_{C\in\textbf{{NC}}^{1},\Delta\subseteq[S]}
, where

\displaystyle G_{C,\Delta}(x,Z_{1},Z_{2},\cdots,Z_{S})\coloneqq C(x)+\sum_{i\in\Delta}Z_{i}

(99)

is a function and
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{S}\in\mathbb{F}
.

We construct a
q
-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme for all
\textbf{{NC}}^{1}
circuits
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\allowbreak\mathsf{Vrfy})

from a 1-bounded certified everlasting FE scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{one}}=\mathsf{ONE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

For
i\in[N]
, generate
(\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MPK}_{i},\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MSK}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{ONE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MPK}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MSK}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},C)
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MSK}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

•

Chooses a uniformly random set
\Gamma\subseteq[N]
of size
tD+1
.

•

Chooses a uniformly random set
\Delta\subseteq[S]
of size
v
.

•

For
i\in\Gamma
, compute
\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{sk}_{C,\Delta,i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ONE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MSK}_{i},G_{C,\Delta})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{sk}_{C}\coloneqq(\Gamma,\Delta,\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{sk}_{C,\Delta,i}\}_{i\in\Gamma})
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},x)
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{MPK}=\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MPK}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

•

For
i\in[\ell]
, pick a random degree
t
polynomial
\mu_{i}(\cdot)
whose constant term is
x[i]
.

•

For
i\in[S]
, pick a random degree
Dt
polynomial
\xi_{i}(\cdot)
whose constant term is
0
.

•

For
i\in[N]
, compute
(\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{CT}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{ONE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{MPK}_{i},(\mu_{1}(i),\cdots,\mu_{\ell}(i),\xi_{1}(i),\cdots,\xi_{S}(i)))
.

•

Output
\mathsf{vk}=\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
and
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{CT}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{C},\mathsf{CT})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sk}_{C}=(\Gamma,\Delta,\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{sk}_{C,\Delta,i}\}_{i\in\Gamma})
and
\mathsf{CT}=\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{CT}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

•

For
i\in\Gamma
, compute
\eta(i)\leftarrow\mathsf{ONE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{sk}_{C,\Delta,i},\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{CT}_{i})
.

•

Output
\eta(0)
.

\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{CT})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{CT}=\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{CT}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

•

For
i\in[N]
, compute
\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{cert}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ONE}.\mathsf{Del}(\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{CT}_{i})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{cert}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
and
\mathsf{cert}=\{\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{cert}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

•

For
i\in[N]
, compute
\top/\bot\leftarrow\mathsf{ONE}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{one}.\mathsf{cert}_{i})
.
If all results are
\top
, output
\top
. Otherwise, output
\bot
.

Correctness:

Verification correctness easily follows from verification correctness of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{one}}
.
Let us show evaluation correctness.
By decryption correctness of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{one}}
, for all
i\in\Gamma
we have

\displaystyle\eta(i)

\displaystyle=G_{C,\Delta}(\mu_{1}(i),\cdots,\mu_{\ell}(i),\xi_{1}(i),\cdots,\xi_{S}(i))

(100)

\displaystyle=C(\mu_{1}(i),\cdots,\mu_{\ell}(i))+\Sigma_{a\in\Delta}\xi_{a}(i).

(101)

Since
|\Gamma|\geq Dt+1
, this means that
\eta
is equal to the degree
Dt
polynomial

\displaystyle\eta(\cdot)=C(\mu_{1}(\cdot),\cdots,\mu_{\ell}(\cdot))+\Sigma_{a\in\Delta}\xi_{a}(\cdot)

(102)

Hence
\eta(0)=C(x_{1},\cdots,x_{\ell})=C(x)
, which means that our construction satisfies evaluation correctness.

Security:

The following two theorems hold.

Theorem 7.13.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{one}}
satisfies the 1-bounded adaptive security,
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}
satisfies the
q
-bounded adaptive security.

Its proof is similar to that of
Theorem

7.14
, and therefore we omit it.

Theorem 7.14.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{one}}
satisfies the 1-bounded certified everlasting adaptive security,

\Sigma_{\mathsf{cefe}}
the
q
-bounded certified everlasting adaptive security.

Its proof is given in
Appendix

E
.

Appendix A
Proof of
Theorem

5.6

Proof of
Theorem

5.6
.

To prove the theorem, let us introduce the sequence of hybrids.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{cence}},\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{rec}\mbox{-}\mathsf{nc}}(\lambda,0)
.
For clarity, we describe the experiment against any adversary
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
, where
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is any QPT adversary and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is any unbounded adversary.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
for all
i\in[n]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.

2.

The challenger sends
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
m\in\mathcal{M}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger generates
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, computes
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha},m[i])
for all
i\in[n]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, and sends
(\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}},(x,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]}))
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

5.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to the challenger and its internal state to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

6.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,\alpha})
for all
i\in[n]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.
If all results are
\top
, the challenger outputs
\top
and sends
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\bot
and sends
\bot
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

7.

\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

8.

If the challenger outputs
\top
, then the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except that
the challenger generates
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1},\allowbreak m[i]\oplus 1)
for all
i\in[n]
instead of computing
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1},m[i])
for all
i\in[n]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except for the following three points.

1.

The challenger generates
x^{*}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
instead of generating
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

For all
i\in[n]
, the challenger generates
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x^{*}[i]},\allowbreak\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x^{*}[i]})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,x^{*}[i]},0)

and
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x^{*}[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x^{*}[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,x^{*}[i]\oplus 1},1)

instead of computing
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x[i]},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x[i]})\allowbreak\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,x[i]},m[i])

and
(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1},m[i]\oplus 1)
.

3.

The challenger sends
(\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}},(x^{*}\oplus m,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x^{*}[i]\oplus m[i]}\}_{i\in[n]}))
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}

instead of sending
(\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}},(x,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]}))
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

It is clear that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
is identical to
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{rec}\mbox{-}\mathsf{nc}}(\lambda,0)
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
is identical to
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{rec}\mbox{-}\mathsf{nc}}(\lambda,1)
.
Hence,
Theorem

5.6
easily follows from the following
Propositions

A.1
and
A.2

(whose proof is given later.).
∎

Proposition A.1.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}}
is certified everlasting IND-CPA secure,
it holds that

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfhyb{0}{}=1]-\Pr[\sfhyb{1}{}=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proposition A.2.

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfhyb{1}{}=1]-\Pr[\sfhyb{2}{}=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof of
Proposition

A.1
.

For the proof, we use
Lemma

A.3
.
We assume that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfhyb{0}{}=1]-\Pr[\sfhyb{1}{}=1]}
is non-negligible,
and construct an adversary
\mathcal{B}
that breaks the security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}},\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda,b)
defined in
Lemma

A.3
.
This contradicts the certified everlasting IND-CPA security of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}}
from
Lemma

A.3
.
Let us describe how
\mathcal{B}
works below.

1.

\mathcal{B}
receives
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
from the challenger of

\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}},\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda,b)
.

2.

\mathcal{B}
sends
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
chooses
m\in\mathcal{M}
and sends
m
to
\mathcal{B}
.

4.

\mathcal{B}
generates
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and sends
(x,m[1],\cdots,m[n],m[1]\oplus 1,\cdots,m[n]\oplus 1)
to the challenger of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}},\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda,b)
.

5.

\mathcal{B}
receives
(\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[n]})
from the challenger of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}},\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda,b)
.

6.

\mathcal{B}
computes
(\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]},\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,x[i]},m[i])
for
i\in[n]
.

7.

\mathcal{B}
sends
(\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}},(x,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]}))
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

8.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{B}
and the internal state to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

9.

\mathcal{B}
sends
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[n]}
to the challenger, and receives
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[n]}
or
\bot
.
If
\mathcal{B}
receives
\bot
, it outputs
\bot
and aborts.

10.

\mathcal{B}
sends
\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

11.

\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

12.

\mathcal{B}
computes
\mathsf{PKE}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{vk}_{i,x[i]},\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{cert}_{i,x[i]})
for all
i\in[n]
.
If all results are
\top
,
\mathcal{B}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.
Otherwise,
\mathcal{B}
outputs
\bot
.

It is clear that
\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathcal{B}|b=0]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]
and
\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathcal{B}|b=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]
.
By assumption,

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfhyb{0}{}=1]-\Pr[\sfhyb{1}{}=1]}
is non-negligible.
Therefore,
\absolutevalue{\Pr[1\la\cB|b=0]-\Pr[1\la\cB|b=1]}
is also non-negligible,
which contradicts the certified everlasting IND-CPA security of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{cepk}}
from
Lemma

A.3
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

A.2
.

It is obvious that the joint probability distribution that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
receives
(\{\mathsf{pke},\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}},\allowbreak(x,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x[i]}\}_{i\in[n]}))
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
is identical to the joint probability distribution that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
receives
(\{\mathsf{pke},\mathsf{CT}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[n],\alpha\in\{0,1\}},\allowbreak(x^{*}\oplus m,\{\mathsf{pke}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,x^{*}[i]\oplus m[i]}\}_{i\in[n]}))
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
.
Hence,
Proposition

A.2
follows.
∎

We use the following lemma for the proof of
Theorem

5.6
and
Theorem

6.7
.
The proof is shown with the standard hybrid argument.
It is also easy to see that a similar lemma holds for IND-CPA security.

Lemma A.3.

Let
s
be some polynomial of the security parameter
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma\coloneqq(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a certified everlasting PKE scheme.
Let us consider the following security experiment

\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda,b)
against
\mathcal{A}
consisting of any QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and any unbounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

1.

The challenge generates
(\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
for all
i\in[s]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
,
and sends
\{\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
chooses
f\in\{0,1\}^{s}
and
(m_{0}[1],m_{0}[2],\cdots,m_{0}[s],m_{1}[1],m_{1}[2],\cdots,m_{1}[s])\in\mathcal{M}^{2s}
,
and sends
(f,m_{0}[1],m_{0}[2],\\
\cdots,m_{0}[s],m_{1}[1],m_{1}[2],\cdots,m_{1}[s])
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger computes

(\mathsf{vk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},m_{b}[i])
for all
i\in[s]
,
and sends
(\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,f[i]}\}_{i\in[s]},\\
\{\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]})
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

4.

At some point,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
\{\mathsf{cert}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]}
to the challenger,
and sends its internal state to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

5.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{cert}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1})
for every
i\in[s]
.
If all results are
\top
, the challenger outputs
\top
, and sends
\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]}
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\bot
, and sends
\bot
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

6.

\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

7.

If the challenger outputs
\top
, then the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

If the
\Sigma
satisfies the certified everlasting IND-CPA security,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[ \expb{\Sigma,\cA}{multi}{cert}{ever}(\secp, 0)=1] - \Pr[ \expb{\Sigma,\cA}{multi}{cert}{ever}(\secp, 1)=1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(103)

for any QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and any unbounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

Proof of
Lemma

A.3
.

Let us consider the following hybrids for
j\in\{0,1,\cdots,s\}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}
:

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
for every
i\in[s]
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, and sends
\{\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
chooses
f\in\{0,1\}^{s}
and
(m_{0}[1],m_{0}[2],\cdots,m_{0}[s],m_{1}[1],m_{1}[2],\cdots,m_{1}[s])\in\mathcal{M}^{2s}
,
and sends
(f,m_{0}[1],m_{0}[2],\cdots,m_{0}[s],m_{1}[1],m_{1}[2],\cdots,m_{1}[s])
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger computes

\displaystyle(\mathsf{vk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},m_{1}[i])

(104)

for
i\in[j]
and

\displaystyle(\mathsf{vk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},m_{0}[i])

(105)

for
i\in\{j+1,j+2,\cdots,s\}
,
and sends
(\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,f[i]}\}_{i\in[s]},\{\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]})
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

4.

At some point,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
\{\mathsf{cert}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]}
to the challenger,
and sends its internal state to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

5.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{cert}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1})
for every
i\in[s]
.
If all results are
\top
, the challenger outputs
\top
, and sends
\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]}
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\bot
, and sends
\bot
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

6.

\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

7.

If the challenger outputs
\top
, then the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

It is clear that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda,0)=1]
and
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{s}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{multi}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}}(\lambda,1)=1]
.
Furthermore, we can show

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfhyb{j}{}=1]-\Pr[\sfhyb{j+1}{}=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(106)

for each
j\in\{0,1,\cdots,s-1\}
.
(Its proof is given below.) From these facts, we obtain
Lemma

A.3
.

Let us show the remaining one. To show it, let us assume that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfhyb{j}{}=1]-\Pr[\sfhyb{j+1}{}=1]}
is non-negligible.
Then, we can construct an adversary
\mathcal{B}
that can break the certified everlasting IND-CPA security of
\Sigma
as follows.

1.

\mathcal{B}
receives
\mathsf{pk}
from the challenger of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda,b)
.

2.

\mathcal{B}
generates
\beta\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and sets
\mathsf{pk}_{j+1,\beta}\coloneqq\mathsf{pk}
.

3.

\mathcal{B}
generates
(\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha},\mathsf{sk}_{i,\alpha})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
for
i\in\{1,\cdots,j,j+2,\cdots,s\}
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, and
(\mathsf{pk}_{j+1,\beta\oplus 1},\mathsf{sk}_{j+1,\beta\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

4.

\mathcal{B}
sends
\{\mathsf{pk}_{i,\alpha}\}_{i\in[s],\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

5.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
chooses
f\in\{0,1\}^{s}
and
(m_{0}[1],m_{0}[2],\cdots,m_{0}[s],m_{1}[1],m_{1}[2],\cdots,m_{1}[s])\in\mathcal{M}^{2s}
,
and sends
(f,m_{0}[1],m_{0}[2],\\
\cdots,m_{0}[s],m_{1}[1],m_{1}[2],\cdots,m_{1}[s])
to the challenger.

6.

If
f[j+1]=\beta
,
\mathcal{B}
aborts the experiment, and outputs
\bot
.

7.

\mathcal{B}
computes

\displaystyle(\mathsf{vk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},m_{1}[i])

(107)

for
i\in[j]
and

\displaystyle(\mathsf{vk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1},m_{0}[i])

(108)

for
i\in\{j+2,\cdots,s\}
.

8.

\mathcal{B}
sends
(m_{0}[j+1],m_{1}[j+1])
to the challenger of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda,b)
.
The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk}_{j+1,f[j+1]\oplus 1},\mathsf{CT}_{j+1,f[j+1]\oplus 1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{j+1,f[j+1]\oplus 1},m_{b}[j+1])
and sends
\mathsf{CT}_{j+1,f[j+1]\oplus 1}
to
\mathcal{B}
.

9.

\mathcal{B}
sends
(\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,f[i]}\}_{i\in[s]},\{\mathsf{CT}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]})
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

10.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
\{\mathsf{cert}_{i}\}_{i\in[s]}
to
\mathcal{B}
, and sends its internal state to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

11.

\mathcal{B}
sends
\mathsf{cert}_{j+1}
to the challenger, and receives
\mathsf{sk}_{j+1,f[j+1]\oplus 1}
or
\bot
from the challenger.
If
\mathcal{B}
receives
\bot
from the challenger, it outputs
\bot
and aborts.

12.

\mathcal{B}
sends
\{\mathsf{sk}_{i,f[i]\oplus 1}\}_{i\in[s]}
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

13.

\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

14.

\mathcal{B}
computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}
for all
\mathsf{cert}_{i}
, and outputs
b^{\prime}
if all results are
\top
. Otherwise,
\mathcal{B}
outputs
\bot
.

Since
\mathsf{pk}_{j+1,\beta}
and
\mathsf{pk}_{j+1,\beta\oplus 1}
are identically distributed,
it holds that

\Pr[f[j+1]=\beta]=\Pr[f[j+1]=\beta\oplus 1]=\frac{1}{2}
.
If
b=0
and
f[j+1]=\beta\oplus 1
,
\mathcal{B}
simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathcal{B}|b=0]

\displaystyle=\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathcal{B}\wedge f[j+1]=\beta\oplus 1|b=0]

(109)

\displaystyle=\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathcal{B}|b=0,f[j+1]=\beta\oplus 1]\cdot\Pr[f[j+1]=\beta\oplus 1]

(110)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}=1].

(111)

If
b=1
and
f[j+1]=\beta\oplus 1
,
\mathcal{B}
simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j+1}
.
Similarly, we have

\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathcal{B}|b=1]=\frac{1}{2}\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{j+1}=1]
.
By assumption,
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfhyb{j}{}=1]-\Pr[\sfhyb{j+1}{}=1]}
is non-negligible, and therefore

\absolutevalue{\Pr[1\la\cB|b=0]-\Pr[1\la\cB|b=1]}
is non-negligible, which contradicts the certified everlasting IND-CPA security of
\Sigma
.
∎

Appendix B

6.7

\widehat{\mathsf{gate}}_{i}=(\bot,w_{a},w_{b},w_{c})
